The Hidden Risks of Building Your Own Delivery Fleet as a Growing Business

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?

Building an in-house delivery fleet may seem like a smart way to reduce costs and gain greater control, but these challenges can quickly impact profitability.

Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.

The invoices from the regional carrier keep getting bigger. For the owner of a growing home furnishings retailer, delivery has become one of the largest expenses outside of inventory, and the complaints that come with it — missed windows, damaged pieces, vague updates — still land on her desk.

So she runs the numbers on three cargo vans and three full-time drivers. On paper, the savings look obvious. What the spreadsheet leaves out is that she is not just buying vans. She is starting a transportation operation inside a company built to sell furniture.

The case for in-house delivery is legitimate. You decide when orders go out and how customers are treated at the door. You depend less on a third party’s capacity during busy weeks, you can coordinate dispatch directly with inventory, and at sufficient volume, you may lower your cost per delivery.

The catch is that an outside carrier bundles its costs into a price per stop. Once you own the operation, those costs scatter. Maintenance appears in repair invoices, insurance in renewal paperwork, depreciation on the balance sheet and recruiting and supervision both in payroll and in the owner’s calendar. Vehicles also sit idle on slow days and break down on busy ones.

In-house delivery is not automatically cheaper. Build a fully loaded cost per delivery that includes idle time, downtime, fuel, insurance, depreciation and management hours, then compare it with outsourced pricing across slow weeks as well as peak ones. A model built on your best month will flatter the fleet.

The same principle applies to broader ecommerce operations. Growing order volumes require companies to reconsider operational processes, technology investments and cost structures.

With two vans, the owner knows who drove which route and when the oil was last changed. That informality works, which is why it tends to persist long after it should be replaced.

At 10, 20 or 50 vehicles, decisions that once lived in one person’s head need to live in a system. Assign ownership for scheduling, maintenance, driver qualification files and performance. In a smaller operation, that may be one capable operations manager, but it must be a named role. Then track a short list of numbers: on-time rate, cost per delivery, vehicle downtime and incidents. Without them, you are managing by anecdote.

This transition reflects a broader challenge of business expansion. Building systems and teams that scale successfully emphasizes the importance of repeatable processes, clear responsibilities and infrastructure that can support increasing operational demands.

A driver is often the only person from your company a customer meets, and also the person operating a heavy vehicle on public roads with your inventory inside. Informal hiring does not scale. A repeatable process does.

Define written qualification criteria, including license status, driving history review where permitted and experience suited to your vehicles, and apply them to every candidate. Onboarding should cover cargo height and clearance, securing loads, customer handoffs and what to do when something goes wrong.

Put your standards for company vehicle use in writing, including phone use, personal use and defect reporting. Some vehicle sizes and operations trigger additional federal or local requirements, so verify what applies to your fleet.

Imagine a company that advertises tight two-hour windows and stacks routes to hit them. It works until a customer isn’t home or a freeway slows. Now the driver is behind, and the easiest way to recover is to speed up, skip a break or glance at a phone.

Fleet safety is partly a scheduling problem. If your routes only work when nothing goes wrong, you have designed in pressure. A few practical fixes help:

Tight windows can remain a selling point where routes are dense and predictable.

Reliable delivery also depends on operational consistency. Efficient workflows and well-organized teams help businesses maintain service standards as demand increases.

Relying on drivers to notice mechanical problems works for a few vehicles. It fails once drivers rotate through different vans and each assumes someone else reported the strange noise.

Set preventive service intervals based on manufacturer guidance and actual use. Require a short pre-trip inspection and make defect reporting penalty-free. Keep records in one place so patterns become visible, such as a van that keeps returning to the shop. Decide in advance that a vehicle with a safety-related problem comes out of service, even on a heavy day.

Then plan for downtime. A spare vehicle, a rental arrangement or an outside carrier on standby keeps one broken van from becoming a week of broken promises. Customers experience your maintenance program as reliability.

Most owners picture an accident as a repair bill. The real cost is wider: employee injuries, injuries to other road users, vehicle replacement, insurance claims and possible premium changes, delayed orders, lost productivity, administrative work and reputational damage. Depending on the circumstances, it can also raise legal questions, which is one reason consistent records matter.

The human side deserves honest attention. Delivery vans and box trucks are often heavier and taller than the passenger cars around them, and the injuries associated with commercial vehicle collisions, including head trauma, spinal damage and fractures, can change someone’s life and disrupt a business for a long time.

A one-page accident procedure, reviewed with every driver, should answer a few questions:

A business that outsources delivery pushes much of its transportation risk onto the carrier. Once you operate your own vehicles, that risk is yours to evaluate.

Work with a qualified broker to review limits, exclusions, covered drivers and how cargo is treated. Pay particular attention to the difference between company-owned vehicles and employees’ personal vehicles used for business, since those situations are often treated differently. Requirements and policy terms vary by jurisdiction and insurer, so confirm the details for your own operation.

Insurance also cannot replace good systems. Coverage helps absorb a loss. It does nothing to prevent the missed inspection, the rushed route or the untrained hire.

Sustainable growth requires systems that can operate consistently without constant intervention from the business owner. This principle is explored in an Entrepreneur article about building repeatable systems for long-term business growth, highlighting the importance of documenting, evaluating and continuously improving business processes.

Owning vehicles buys control, but control only pays off through discipline: clear ownership, consistent hiring, realistic schedules, preventive maintenance, an accident plan and a sober view of cost. The businesses that succeed with in-house delivery do so because they built a system around their vans, not because they bought good ones.

The invoices from the regional carrier keep getting bigger. For the owner of a growing home furnishings retailer, delivery has become one of the largest expenses outside of inventory, and the complaints that come with it — missed windows, damaged pieces, vague updates — still land on her desk.

So she runs the numbers on three cargo vans and three full-time drivers. On paper, the savings look obvious. What the spreadsheet leaves out is that she is not just buying vans. She is starting a transportation operation inside a company built to sell furniture.

The case for in-house delivery is legitimate. You decide when orders go out and how customers are treated at the door. You depend less on a third party’s capacity during busy weeks, you can coordinate dispatch directly with inventory, and at sufficient volume, you may lower your cost per delivery.

Morgan Stanley Starts Tokenizing US Stocks and Treasuries via DTCC

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Home » Crypto Market News » Morgan Stanley Starts Tokenizing US Stocks and Treasuries via DTCC

The bank confirmed it is among the first financial institutions to adopt DTCC’s tokenization service.

Morgan Stanley is taking a major step into modern market infrastructure by tokenizing U.S. equities and Treasuries through the Depository Trust & Clearing Corporation’s (DTCC) new Tokenization Service (DTS).

Following the platform’s official launch on October 28, the bank confirmed on X that it is actively using the system to create digital representations of traditional real-world assets.

This move brings Wall Street’s largest institutions closer to fully digital, on-chain trading infrastructure. More than 20 major financial firms—including JPMorgan and Citi—have signed agreements to participate

In its official X post, Morgan Stanley said it is “among the first banks to tokenize real-world assets” following the DTS launch. The bank said the service converts US equities and US Treasurys into tokenized form.

We are thrilled to announce that Morgan Stanley is among the first banks to tokenize real-world assets following the launch of the @The_DTCC Tokenization Service (DTS), converting U.S. Equity and U.S. Treasuries to tokenized form. Launched on October 28, DTS creates tokenized… pic.twitter.com/wUNMnYB5gC

According to the announcement, DTS creates tokenized representations of securities held in custody at the Depository Trust Company (DTC). Morgan Stanley said the goal is greater liquidity, collateral mobility, and capital efficiency.

DTS has been live since October 28 on the Canton Network and Hyperledger Besu blockchain. The service initially supports selected companies in the Russell 1000 index, ETFs tracking major indices, and US Treasury bills, notes, and bonds. The tokenized assets are designed to support 24/7 settlement, liquidity, and use as collateral.

According to DTCC, approximately $30 trillion in eligible securities may be covered by the initial rollout. More than 20 firms, including JPMorgan and Citi, have signed on to the service.

The tokenized real-world asset (RWA) market is worth $39.4 billion, with U.S. Treasuries accounting for more than $14.2 billion, according to RWA.xyz.

This institutional migration onto blockchain rails is made possible by clear regulatory framework updates. The U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) issued a no-action letter to the DTCC, allowing its subsidiary DTC to operate tokenization services on approved blockchains under a three-year framework.

The SEC also approved rule updates allowing Nasdaq to roll out tokenized settlement options for Russell 1000 equities and major index-tracking ETFs, while work continues on broader digital asset innovation standards.

As the central backbone of U.S. market clearing, the DTCC oversees more than $114 trillion in assets under custody. Its adoption of public-enterprise hybrid blockchains marks a decisive shift from experimental pilot programs to fully regulated, mainstream financial operations.

Poll: Is Phantom Blade Zero Too Hard?

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Now that Phantom Blade Zero's been out for a good few days, I thought it'd be interesting to check in on how everyone's finding the experience in terms of difficulty.

This has been a constant topic ever since the game was first announced, with onlookers initially questioning whether it would be a hard-as-nails Soulslike.

However, developer S-GAME has continually stressed that Phantom Blade Zero is not in the same category as FromSoftware's punishing action RPGs; it's got difficulty settings, and you don't lose any resources upon death.

Indeed, Phantom Blade Zero is a fairly forgiving game overall — at least in terms of structure. Where it presents a challenge is in mastering its combat, which often demands careful defensive timing and therefore knowledge of your opponent.

Just before launch, I wrote a sort-of-guide on the title's difficulty, which concludes that while Phantom Blade Zero isn't brutally difficult — unless you're playing on the hardest Hellwalker setting — it's still not an 'easy' game to beat.

Feature How Difficult Is Phantom Blade Zero?

A helpful overview of the game's challenge

Guide Phantom Blade Zero: Best Weapons and Where to Find Them

More experienced action game players will likely push through the adventure with relatively few deaths on the easier 'Wayfarer' and standard 'Pathbreaker' options, but others could still struggle to progress at certain points.

With that in mind, I've seen somewhat mixed takes on the game's general level of challenge, even across this site's own comments sections.

As you can probably imagine, some believe that it's actually very manageable — particularly when compared to its perceived peers, while others have expressed frustration with certain combat mechanics and difficulty spikes.

This kind of divide was always going to manifest, I think, but that's why the topic makes for an intriguing poll.

So, what do you think of the difficulty in Phantom Blade Zero? Is it just right? Actually easy? Or maybe way too hard? Vote in our polls, and then explain your stance in the comments section below.

Robert (or Rob if you're lazy) is an assistant editor of Push Square, and has been a fan of PlayStation since the 90s, when Tekken 2 introduced him to the incredible world of video games. He still takes his fighting games seriously, but RPGs are his true passion. The Witcher, Persona, Dragon Quest, Mass Effect, Final Fantasy, Trails, Tales — he's played 'em all. A little too much, some might say.

Bosses can be a different story, though. I find them much more challenging, and I really appreciate the checkpoints between phases. I rarely enjoy boss fights; I come to these kinds of games for the level design and exploration, and bosses just get in the way of that (so much so that I rarely bother with final bosses, knowing there’s nothing left to discover beyond them).

Where sekiro was difficult, there was precision to it and parry timing. Here it can just be a mess on screen. Toward the latter period of the game you are often fighting two or three bosses at a time all with the same messiness to them. The game then becomes a mess on screen and forces a lot more masking of dodge, parry, or strike with loads of ***** all over the screen that induces a bit of button mashing. Just missing the precision.

Many victories can then feel less like overcoming an adversary via skill, and more one of luck and a boss attack rng.

That aside the regular enemies are extremely easy until the last third of the game. Then they become far more fun to fight.

The exploration, side quests and metroidvania structure is top notch though. Despite it having unnecessary shortcomings, I'm still very much enjoying the game and can't put it down, 🤣

Sky: Predicted Milan XI vs. Ferencvaros – rotation galore for Amorim at San Siro

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

After the game against Inter, AC Milan were expected to rotate and they certainly have, based on the predicted XI to face Ferencvaros.

A win like the one against Inter takes a lot out of players, that is without considering the October which they’ve had, too. So, the standard rotation in the Europa League was expected to be increased, with reports suggesting a four-year drop in the team’s average age.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

The starting XI is expected to have a very youthful feel, which most hoped would be the case in midweek games anyway. Nevertheless, it’s not a ‘throwaway’ game; it’s just as important to Ruben Amorim as the derby.

So, the changes start in defence, where Lorenzo Torriani replaces the suspended Mike Maignan, as Sky reports (seen below). Koni De Winter should be the only player to retain a starting spot in the backline, with Filippo Terracciano and Davide Bartesaghi coming in, whilst Sankhoun Diawara drops back into the back four.

In midfield, Yunus Musah will partner Ardon Jashari and they will be supported by Adrien Rabiot, who will sit ahead of the younger duo.

A vaguely youthful front line sees Omari Hutchinson and Alphadjo Cisse start on the flanks, with Goncalo Ramos starting in the middle again.

Glioblastoma’s Chaos Follows Predictable Rules

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A dual-study breakthrough published in Nature by researchers at EMBL, the German Cancer Research Center (DKFZ), and the Wellcome Sanger Institute demonstrates that the notorious cellular plasticity of glioblastoma is not random. By building the most comprehensive multi-omic and spatial molecular atlas of glioblastoma to date across more than one million patient-derived cells, the team uncovered stereotyped transition trajectories between distinct cell states.

Leveraging a novel deep learning framework named scDORI, the researchers identified key master regulators, including the safeguard factor MYT1L. Overexpressing MYT1L locked malignant cells into low-plasticity states, blunting invasiveness and extending survival in preclinical models, establishing a therapeutic blueprint for cell-state reprogramming.

Source: EMBL / Wellcome Sanger Institute / DKFZ / Nature

Glioblastoma represents the most common, aggressive, and universally fatal primary malignant brain tumor in adults. Despite intensive multimodal interventions, combining maximal surgical resection, temozolomide chemotherapy, and localized radiotherapy, median survival remains approximately 15 months from diagnosis, with recurrence nearly inevitable.

A primary driver of therapeutic failure is extreme intratumoral heterogeneity. Within a single tumor mass, malignant cells exist in a diverse spectrum of cellular identities: some resemble primitive neural stem cells, others mimic mature neurons or glia, and others enter a hyper-aggressive, injury-response state. Furthermore, these cells exhibit dynamic plasticity, shifting between states in response to microenvironmental stress or pharmacological pressure.

For decades, oncology dogma maintained that this state-shifting was stochastic and chaotic, creating an unpredictable moving target that easily evades single-agent therapies.

Now, two coordinated studies published back-to-back in Nature overturn this view. Led by international research consortia at the European Molecular Biology Laboratory (EMBL), the German Cancer Research Center (DKFZ), and the Wellcome Sanger Institute, scientists have proven that glioblastoma cell transitions obey strict, predictable mathematical rules—and that these transitions can be artificially reprogrammed to disarm tumor malignancy.

The breakthrough emerged from a foundational effort to map glioblastoma biology without relying on prior assumptions. Supported by a Wellcome Leap grant, Dr. Omer Bayraktar, Group Leader at the Wellcome Sanger Institute and professor at the UCL Cancer Institute, spearheaded the construction of a comprehensive multi-omic patient atlas.

Bayraktar’s team profiled DNA mutations, epigenetic modifications, and single-cell RNA expression across fresh tumor resections from 12 patients. By combining single-cell sequencing with high-resolution spatial transcriptomics, the researchers uncovered an organized spatial hierarchy:

“By integrating the single-cell data with spatial transcriptomic data, we found that there is a meticulous and very specific spatial architecture of cancer cell states,” explained Dr. Bayraktar. “And this was present in every single glioblastoma tumour in every single patient we studied.”

To interpret the immense regulatory complexity of this dataset, Dr. Oliver Stegle’s computational team at EMBL Heidelberg and DKFZ developed scDORI (single-cell Deep learning for Operator and Regulator Inference).

Rather than viewing the tumor’s staggering genetic variation as experimental noise, scDORI leveraged this natural diversity across more than one million patient-derived cells. The deep learning system compared how transcriptional regulators and downstream target genes co-varied, deducing the gene regulatory networks that govern how glioblastoma cells decide their fate.

“A tumour generates an almost endless number of variations on its own regulatory programme,” noted Dr. Stegle. “Instead of seeing this heterogeneity only as an obstacle, we can use AI to learn from it and extract the rules that determine how cells choose their fate.”

Armed with the regulatory circuit maps generated by scDORI, Dr. Moritz Mall’s group at DKFZ investigated whether manipulating these molecular switches could override tumor plasticity.

The analysis identified a crucial class of molecular “safeguards”—transcription factors that act as developmental brakes, arresting cells in stable, low-plasticity states and preventing them from shifting into therapy-resistant or invasive phenotypes.

Focusing on one such safeguard regulator, the neural transcription factor MYT1L, Mall’s laboratory experimentally overexpressed the protein in patient-derived glioblastoma stem cells:

“One of our premises has always been that when we understand these rules, we can not only observe what the cells are doing, but we can also change what they are doing,” said Dr. Mall. “Our approach predicts the regulators for all of the major tumour cell states, and we tested those predictions experimentally. We were able to steer tumour cells into specific states by manipulating just a handful of regulators.”

While clinical translation remains in development, the ability to predict and manipulate tumor cell identity marks a paradigm shift from cytotoxic eradication to targeted cellular reprogramming. By treating cellular heterogeneity as a rule-based computational problem, the researchers have established an AI-driven framework that can now be applied to unravel and target therapy evasion across breast, lung, and metastatic cancers.

Why has glioblastoma historically been impossible to cure?

Glioblastoma displays extreme cellular heterogeneity and plasticity. Malignant cells exist in multiple distinct states (ranging from stem-like to injury-like states) and dynamically shift identities under therapeutic attack, enabling subpopulations to survive chemotherapy and radiation.

What did the new Nature studies discover about glioblastoma plasticity?

The researchers discovered that glioblastoma cell transitions are not random. Instead, cells follow stereotyped, predictable trajectories between states that occupy distinct spatial niches within the tumor tissue.

What is scDORI and how did it identify the MYT1L regulator?

scDORI is a deep-learning method developed by EMBL and DKFZ that infers gene regulatory networks across single-cell datasets. By analyzing over one million cells, scDORI mapped the molecular switches controlling cell fate, identifying the transcription factor MYT1L as a safeguard that locks cells into less malignant, low-plasticity states.

Decoding the regulatory programs governing glioblastoma plasticity

Glioblastoma is incurable and resistant to therapy despite genomic stability under treatment, suggesting that non-genetic cellular plasticity is a central driver of progression. Whether such plasticity reflects stochastic state switching or is governed by predictable gene regulation has remained unclear.

Here we introduce scDORI (single-cell deep multi-omic regulatory inference)—a deep-learning framework that infers enhancer-driven gene-regulatory networks at single-cell resolution—and apply it to single-nucleus multiome profiles of more than one million cells from primary glioblastomas.

Integrating computational inference with systematic perturbation screens across patient-derived models, we show that a structured regulatory program with an asymmetric interplay of activators and repressors governs cellular plasticity in glioblastoma.

The resulting transition landscape is hierarchical and predictable, but permits cross-lineage trajectories that are absent from normal neurodevelopment. Within this landscape, low-plasticity states such as the neuronal-like state are not stable differentiation end points but actively maintained configurations, stabilized by a network of safeguard repressors that silence alternative fates.

We identify MYT1L as the dominant member of this network, and show that it binds to and represses master regulators of every alternative state to consolidate neuronal-like identity. MYT1L restricted plasticity even under standard-of-care therapy and suppressed tumour growth and invasion in vivo. Conversely, loss of MYT1L reactivated plasticity and accelerated malignant features, indicating that low-plasticity identity requires continuous repression.

These findings reframe malignant cell state stability as a balance between activation and continuous repression, and nominate safeguard repressors as candidate therapeutic targets that are distinct from differentiation-inducing activators, with potential relevance across cancers in which phenotypic switching drives therapy resistance.

Fragmentary Order Looks Like Sci-Fi Escape from Tarkov, And You Can Play It This Year

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Covering the video games industry since 2017, with experience in news, articles, and reviews. Currently a writer at DualShockers and gaming magazines.

If you've ever wondered what Escape from Tarkov would look like if its brutal firefights took place somewhere beyond Earth, Nikita Buyanov might have just the answer. The game director behind the popular hardcore shooter has finally offered a proper look at his next project, and it seems he's not planning to make things any easier for players.

Called Fragmentary Order, the upcoming sci-fi tactical shooter just received its first gameplay reveal, featuring an impressive 14 minutes of in-engine footage. And if you're curious to see what Buyanov has been working on, you can watch the full gameplay reveal on YouTube!

Escape From Tarkov's creator reveals that his upcoming game is 'almost there.'

Set across the Solar System in 2251, Fragmentary Order imagines a future where warfare has become an outsourced business. So, instead of putting their own lives on the line, players control remotely operated clones known as RepEnts, which are deployed across large combat zones to carry out all sorts of dangerous missions.

Interestingly, losing one of these clones isn't necessarily the end of the road. In this world, death is simply another business expense, but that doesn't mean players can afford to be careless. The game features large-scale environments, changing environmental conditions, vehicles, drones, and advanced technological systems, with squads of up to four players expected to coordinate their operations.

If you've ever wondered what Escape from Tarkov would look like if its brutal firefights took place somewhere beyond Earth, Nikita Buyanov might have just the answer.

Buyanov is also taking a more grounded approach to its sci-fi setting (as you should expect from the same mind that created Escape From Tarkov). Rather than relying on futuristic gimmicks like wall-hacks, the upcoming hardcore shooter features electromagnetic sensors that can expose players' positions, meaning every encounter requires careful planning and an understanding of the battlefield. In other words, running around without thinking twice probably won't get you very far!

Battlestate Games boss Nikita Buyanov opens up about his creative vision for his upcoming game, Fragmentary Order.

Alongside the gameplay reveal, Buyanov also announced “Prime Batch”, a paid program that gives players access to the game's upcoming closed testing sessions. So yes, some dedicated fans won't have to wait until the game's full release to get their hands on it, as closed testing sessions are expected to begin later this year.

There are two available tiers: Candidate ($44.99), which includes access to alpha sessions and major technical playtests, and Founder ($79.99), which adds earlier access to experimental builds, roadmap updates, and a private community with direct developer access. The first closed alpha wave is planned for later this year, and you can check out more about it on this page.

For now, Fragmentary Order doesn't have a confirmed release date, but its first gameplay showcase has certainly given fans plenty to discuss. If Buyanov manages to capture the same long-term appeal that made Escape from Tarkov so popular while introducing a compelling sci-fi setting, his next project could be one worth keeping an eye on.

Fragmentary Order is a next-generation multiplayer combat tactical simulator.

Meet the 20 first-place apps from RevenueCat’s Shipaton 2026

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A cozy habit tracker, a smarter snippet library, and a rooster that wants you out of bed: this year’s Shipaton winners offer plenty of new apps to explore.

RevenueCat’s annual app-building competition drew 3,985 project submissions in 2026, according to its winner announcement. Across the 20 first-place categories, developers tackled everyday frustrations, built inventive games, and found new ways to connect people.

Here’s a look at every first-place winner:

Read RevenueCat’s full winner announcement

Keeping a routine is easier when there’s something to look forward to. Mio pairs self-care and habit tracking with a pixel companion that grows as you make progress. Check off habits, earn coins, and unlock new companions and collectibles, with friends and groups adding a social reason to keep going.

Mio took Shipaton’s Grand Prize, with RevenueCat highlighting its revenue and growth in a crowded habit-tracking market.

That dog you met on your morning walk can become part of your collection. Boop identifies cats and dogs and turns their photos into stickers for a digital book, giving pet lovers a playful reason to pay attention to the animals around them.

Creator Taiwo Omisore’s “100 Boops” challenge took the app into the real world, where printed pet portraits and conversations with owners helped shape the product. That hands-on approach earned Boop first place in #BuildInPublic.

If you have fond memories of futuristic arcade racers, Nitelore has a familiar appeal: hovering vehicles, neon tracks, and plenty of speed. Chase your own ghost in time trials, take on combat races, or compete online.

Free tracks offer a starting point, while paid unlocks and daily and weekly challenges give players more to explore. RevenueCat’s Best Game judges singled out the art direction, effects, and haptics.

Explaining how you see color can be surprisingly difficult. See as Me: Colorblind makes that conversation visual, using a split-screen camera to compare different types and degrees of color blindness. A shareable link lets someone else see the comparison without installing the app.

Developer Javier Jiménez drew on his own experience with protanopia. That personal perspective helped the app earn the RevenueCat Peace Prize.

Clucky’s rooster is determined to get you out of bed. Turning off an alarm can require solving a problem, moving around, or finding an object, bringing a little friction to the temptation to snooze.

Its personality runs through the entire interface, including a time picker that uses the rooster’s scrolling eyes and yellow beak. That consistent character, animation, and attention to detail earned Clucky the RevenueCat Design Award.

Adversary starts with a familiar challenge: flip cards and find matching pairs. The catch is a hidden Danger card with no matching partner. Reveal it, and an ad becomes part of the board you’re trying to remember.

That idea earned Niv Ben-porath’s game the Catvertising Award. Players can continue with ads after an initial ad-free period, or choose a permanent unlock that removes ads and adds difficulty options.

Put a few phones on the table, and Bubble Games turns their screens into a shared game board. Objects move between devices as friends compete in minigames, including a virtual hot potato.

Built by four software engineering students at Université Laval, the Next Gen Award winner uses the phones people already have to create a shared activity. Its Shipaton submission includes ten minigames. The winner announcement links to the Android release on Google Play.

GRIDLOCK takes a game everyone knows and adds up to four players, online rooms, and eleven special moves. The result offers more ways to disrupt an opponent’s plans than the familiar three-by-three board.

Its HAMM Award win recognizes how the business model fits the game: a shared Credits currency connects rewarded ads, optional packs, and a subscription, while core modes remain free. Players can choose the option that fits how often they play.

Just Add begins with a craving. Type what you feel like eating, describe it aloud, or take a photo, and the app suggests three meals built around it, adding protein, fiber, and healthy fats to round out the plate.

Shopping support, hands-free cooking, and remembered preferences help carry the idea through to dinner. The winner of Abbey’s Kitchen’s nutrition challenge also impressed judges with a dinner-host feature that accounts for other people’s preferences.

The reply you send every week, the PDF you keep hunting for, and the image you paste repeatedly can all live in SnipStash. It organizes reusable text, images, and files across iPhone, iPad, and Mac, with a keyboard, Mac quick palette, hotkeys, and Shortcuts bringing them within reach.

Private iCloud sync connects the library across devices. Its speed and native interface helped it win Christopher Lawley’s productivity challenge.

Knowing what you want to say and saying it aloud are different things. Rehearse – Hard Conversations gives you an AI counterpart to practice with before a difficult workplace discussion.

Choose from 33 prepared scenarios or bring your own situation, then review feedback on clarity, empathy, assertiveness, and structure. A prep card helps you take the useful points into the real conversation. The app won Leadership Heather’s career-coaching challenge for its focused, straightforward approach.

You see a game in a short video, mean to look it up later, and forget its name. Game Magnet lets you share the clip to identify the games and save them to your backlog.

Processing happens in the background, with a notification when the result is ready. Shelves, ratings, and ranked lists help organize what comes next. The app won Mr Lewis Blogs Gaming’s challenge by making discovery and keeping track of games easier.

FeelGood: Movement Menu starts with a short check-in about your energy, available time, and how you want to feel. It then offers a small menu of movement, with an appetizer, main, and optional sides.

The options span yoga, Pilates, strength, walking, stretching, and breathing. Rest is part of the experience, too. Yusra Salam and Ameena Malik’s app won Simone Sharice’s yoga and fitness challenge, with judges praising its calm, inviting design.

BoatBrawl brings arcade boat battles to a group of up to eight players. Steer, fire, and scramble through interactive arenas, with bots and shared-screen play offering more ways to join in.

Rehaan Cubes and Ashwin K M used Kotlin Multiplatform and Compose Multiplatform to bring the game across phones and computers, including iOS, Android, Mac, Windows, and the web. Its smooth browser play helped it take JetBrains’ Ship Kotlin Everywhere award.

Trazo: Golf Ball Tracer adds a ball-flight line to your own golf videos. Select the ball twice to start, and the processing runs on the phone.

Julian Praest’s app won Most Viral App for the way its team introduced the product through organic content. Golf humor and practical advice set up the tracer reveal, making the finished video part of the pitch. For golfers, the appeal is simple: make the shot easier to follow when you share it.

One Piece Card Game collectors know that similar-looking cards can be very different finds. Grandfolio helps identify artwork variants, manage duplicates, and track collection prices and progress.The Best App for Galaxy winner impressed judges with layouts that adapt to folded, tabletop, and open-screen use. There’s an App Store release for Apple users, too, making this a relevant discovery even if your collection starts on an iPhone.

Let’s Get Support by 3Dadz is designed for partners, parents, and friends affected by someone else’s addiction. Check-ins, journaling, boundary-setting tools, and local resources give those supporters a place to focus on their own needs.

Joshua Burgess and Aaryn B. built the app from community conversations in British Columbia, using Replit to turn the idea into a live product. It won Replit’s Idea to Income award. The announcement links to its Android release.

PoquitoTalk: Bocas del Toro turns English into local Spanish voice notes for WhatsApp and helps translate the replies. The recipient doesn’t need to install the app, keeping the conversation in a tool people already use.

Creator Dorien Van den Abbeele drew on years of life in Bocas del Toro. Its Growth Loop Award recognizes extensive growth experiments and an honest account of what the measurements could show. The announcement links to Google Play.

Parky! combines wait times, dining availability, and memories from Disney and Universal visits. Built by former Disney Cast Member Chase Van Derrick, it aims to offer useful help throughout a park day.

Dining alerts can become conversations on the Lock Screen, where you can ask about another time or restaurant. Alongside queue Live Activities and recap messages, those interactive notifications helped Parky! win the Keep Them Coming Back Award.

In Your Own Words offers short, real-world speaking scenarios and feedback on pace, hesitation, and filler words. Speech analysis takes place on the phone, giving users a way to practice the words before they need them.

Natasha Wilson’s app won the Funnel Vision Award for connecting a web quiz, a relevant practice deck, and a web purchase that unlocks access in the app. The introduction to the product begins before the download.

Friendpire turns the idea of a board-game night with friends into a shared hex-tile world. Gather resources, build farms and docks, and grow an empire together. RevenueCat employee Erik MacInnis built the game to make it easier to stay connected with friends who rarely find time to play.

Friendpire won the separate Conflict of Interest Award, which was open to RevenueCat and sponsor employees and judged by Shipaton participants. It is included here as an optional sidebar and is not part of the 20 first-place count above.

From a useful tool for the workday to a game for your next night in, Shipaton’s winners show how much can come from taking an idea all the way to a shipped app. Explore the winners above, or browse RevenueCat’s full announcement for more apps from the competition.

Scientists Build Biocomputer That Runs on Bacteria to Solve Hard Problems

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The study relied on the bacterium E. coli and V. natriegens to run the calculations.

Move over, microchips. The next generation of supercomputing might just run on living bacteria. For 20 years, an international team of scientists has been developing a biocomputer that uses bacteria to process information. Scientists from McGill University in Montreal announced their findings on Nov. 3 and published a paper detailing the results earlier this year in Nature.

Researchers from Canada, Europe, the United Kingdom and Australia contributed to this work, which combines biology with computer science and engineering. The researchers say the resulting biocomputer could be used to solve problems that give standard computers a hard time.

“Electronic (sequential) computers are not meant, or designed, to solve decision trees,” Dan Nicolau, a McGill bioengineering professor and senior author for the research paper, told CNET via email.

Nicolau noted that if electronic computers are to solve these types of problems, the computers will need a lot of RAM.

The study relied on the bacterium Escherichia coli and Vibrio natriegens to run the calculations. The researchers used E. coli because it moves through fluid in a predictable, measurable way and V. natriegens because it reproduces very quickly.

By using live bacteria rather than digital processing, the team says the biocomputer can solve big, complex problems at a small fraction of the memory and resources that regular computers require.

The researchers tested the biocomputer with a seemingly simple math problem called the Subset Sum Problem, which famously becomes exponentially harder as the number of inputs increases.

When the Subset Sum Problem inputs grow too large, the problem is known to give traditional computers issues, such as taking centuries, or even millions of years, to solve the problem.

The researchers translated the problem into a graph that branches off into each possible combination of inputs. Next, the team created a tiny physical structure that mirrored the Subset Sum Problem graph.

Inside the structure, they built a network of tunnels to mirror the graph branches. Each fork in the tunnels represents a decision point, and every possible route through the tunnels represents a different solution to the problem.

The researchers unleashed bacteria, which rapidly reproduce, inside the tunnels. The fast-growing bacteria moved through the tunnels, exploring many routes to the solution simultaneously.

Compared to digital processing, the bacteria are slow. But rather than tackling a problem sequentially, as traditional computers do, the bacteria can examine many solutions at once. And if you need more computing power, bacterial reproduction within the tunnels creates additional processing units to help solve the problem.

The team used automated image analysis to follow the bacteria through the correct tunnels. In this way, the researchers were able to tell which paths through the network satisfied the problem’s constraints.

The researchers were also able to adjust temperatures to prevent bacteria overcrowding while traveling through the network.

Enterprising fans have figured out how to run Doom, the groundbreaking 1993 first-person shooter video game, on everything from E. coli to a thermostat. Nicolau says Doom could probably run on the new system, with an important caveat concerning the biocomputer’s size.

“(The biocomputer) must be huge!” Nicolau said. Because bacteria make slow processors, you would need a massive biocomputer to host enough bacteria to run a video game.

But because no RAM is needed, Nicolau said biocomputing could help to solve the global RAM shortage, and even broaden our understanding of what a computer could be.

“A computer is a device which accepts (input) numbers and gets out (output) numbers. Full stop,” he said. “All other uses of the word computer (are) from our point of view, well, poetical.”

2027 Colors of the Year: The Shades Defining Design

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Each fall, the design world turns its attention to the year ahead: What colors and trends will shape the next 12 months? Leading paint manufacturers offer their predictions through annual Color of the Year selections, drawing on evolving aesthetics, cultural influences, and shifting attitudes toward the spaces we inhabit.

But these selections do more than anticipate what’s next. Over time, Colors of the Year have become influential trendsetters in their own right, inspiring everything from interiors and architecture to furniture, fashion, and product design. And while each brand brings its own perspective to the process, their collective choices often reveal broader shifts in how we think about color.

For 2027, greens, earthy browns, and muted blues feature prominently, reflecting a continued interest in nature, comfort, and spaces that feel personal and lived-in. As this year’s selections are unveiled, we’re taking a closer look at the shades making their mark, the thinking behind each choice, and what they might tell us about the year to come.

Drawing on the raw, natural beauty of materials, Cottage Door by Valspar brings a sense of familiarity to the home. This muted blue reflects the growing shift toward intentional living, highlighting design choices as a form of self-reflection. Not defined by seasons or fleeting trends, Cottage Door seamlessly adapts to the natural world’s changing color palettes. Its understated character embraces the beauty of imperfection, celebrating the memories and lived-in details that personalize a space.

Behr’s 2027 Color of the Year, Grounded, invites the outdoors in. Merging mossy olive hues with brown undertones, the shade evokes a sense of stability. Grounded blurs the line between interiors and the natural world, transforming living spaces into natural sanctuaries.

Celery by Sherwin-Williams introduces a fresh perspective to interiors, creating an organic, balanced environment within a soace. Its airy, pastel hue combines tranquility and vitality, emphasizing the relationship between interior design and well-being. Whether used as a quiet background or prominent accent, Celery brings a restorative quality to interiors, striking a balance between organic neutrals and contemporary color palettes.

Stepping beyond momentary trends, Benjamin Moore’s Webster Green carries the sophistication of classic design. Balancing tradition with contemporary appeal, this elegant green reflects the present while looking toward the future, bringing character to any room.

Artifact, Glidden’s choice for 2027 Color of the Year, offers a fresh perspective on interiors through a calm, muted aqua hue, refreshing the home without the need for a complete renovation. A versatile backdrop for personal expression, Artifact reimagines the minimalist white trend, preserving the simplicity while introducing a delicate wash of color.

Paint and wallpaper brand Little Greene selected Marle as its 2027 Color of the Year. Mirroring the return of understated browns in both fashion and interior design, this earthy hue is designed to foster comfort and soften the surrounding space. Describing the shade as “sumptuous and impactful, yet relaxed and grounded,” Little Greene’s Creative Director Ruth Mottershead highlights the balance between richness and restraint that defines the color.

Quiet Peace by California Paints encourages us to slow down and take in our surroundings. This deep, moody blue creates a sense of calm while leaving room for self-expression through design. Rather than simply decorating a room, Quiet Peace helps create a thoughtful relationship between space, color, and the people who inhabit it, offering a welcome refuge from the chaos of the outside world.

Dunn-Edwards announced French Press Coffee as its 2027 Color of the Year, predicting the rise of desaturated, atmospheric hues in the coming year. This silky, rich brown evokes luxury and history, cultivating intimacy through warmth and understated sophistication.

As the name suggests, Dutch Boy Paints’ Color of the Year, Deep Rooted, aims to create a grounded, authentic, and dependable environment within a space. With its earthy terracotta hue and warm undertones, Deep Rooted encourages personalized, lived-in environments.

Ace Hardware’s paint brand Clark+Kensington chose Vantage Point, a radiant blue shade that brings a crisp, refreshing perspective to interiors and adds uplifting energy to everyday spaces.

Inspired by the natural minerals and pigments found in Italian clay deposits, Graham & Brown’s Rose Earth connects modern spaces with historical painting practices. This subtle pink, enriched by a warm terracotta undertone, brings centuries-old influences into contemporary interiors.

C2’s 2027 Color of the Year, Pueblo, looks beyond simply finding a retreat and explores what it means to feel truly alive within a space. With its earthy pink and mauve tones, Pueblo encourages physical interaction with our surroundings, transforming interiors into spaces where people actively engage with their environment rather than simply retreat from it.

Abdul El-Sayed Is Winning at Watches, Too

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Michigan voters made history Tuesday night by electing Abdul El-Sayed to the US Senate, as part of a greater blue wave that swept the nation. The 44-year-old former epidemiologist is set to become America’s first Muslim senator—and, in a slightly more trivial milestone, might also be the first real-deal watch nerd to reach the Capitol.

During his victory speech, the senator-elect sported a Grand Seiko Spring Drive Chronograph (ref. SBGC249), a limited-edition piece from the Japanese watchmaker that pairs a GMT (which tracks the time across multiple time zones) and a chronograph (i.e. a stopwatch function). Politicians tend to go one of two ways with their timepieces: Either they’ll cosplay as a man of the people with an affordable Timex or Casio, or they’ll sport a luxury watch with historical pedigree—like the Rolex “President” Day-Date, worn by several commanders-in-chief—to make themselves appear ready for higher office. An obscure Grand Seiko fits neither of those categories.

Instead, El-Sayed’s Grand Seiko is a burly sport model made by one of the world’s most elegant watchmakers. At 43.5mm, it’s hefty, a good fit for the senator-elect’s beefed-up forearms. It’s quite complicated, with several different functions, and a dial as busy as Little Caesars Arena during a Detroit Pistons game. It costs $12,400, but you’d have to be very deep into the hobby to recognize its value.

What I find so compelling about El-Sayed’s choice is that it doesn’t appear to be masquerading as some political symbol. It’s simply the excellent wristwear of an honest-to-goodness collector. “Folks who know me know I’m a sucker for automatic watches and sort of classic watches,” El-Sayed said in an interview with LGBT Detroit in May.

Throughout his campaign, he’s worn a wide range of pieces. His recent go-to appears to be a Mühle-Glashütte S.A.R. Rescue-Timer, a great-looking and obscure German tool watch. He also owns the U-1 SE, a large dive watch from the German independent brand Sinn.

Not all of El-Sayed’s timepieces are devoid of political meaning, however. The senator-elect has worn an Omega Speedmaster, the watch worn by American astronauts Buzz Aldrin and Neil Armstrong when they first set foot on the moon. That’s about as patriotic as a watch can get without excerpting the Declaration of Independence on the dial. Naturally, he also owns a Shinola Runwell, from the brand based in El-Sayed’s native Detroit. El-Sayed is very aware of what a certain watch can signal about the wearer. In his 2020 book, Healing Politics: A Doctor’s Journey into the Heart of Our Political Epidemic, he wrote about how seeing Rolex billboards along a highway prompted him to reflect on income inequality: “How many people who drive this road can actually afford one of those?”

But when did I realize that El-Sayed was a true-blue watch guy? It wasn’t the dialed-in collection of interesting tool watches that did it, but his appearance at a rally on Sunday. He wasn’t wearing a timepiece at all, surprisingly. Which meant that there, on his wrist, was the mark of a true collector: a watch tan.

Pangram’s new models spot AI fakes in images and video, it says

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The AI text detection startup adds heatmaps, video uploads and browser alerts, but its accuracy figures are self-reported.

Pangram’s demo: a real photo of a restaurant whose wall mural is AI-generated (left), the Image 1.0 heatmap (centre) and the overlay (right).

Pangram launched Pangram Image 1.0 for AI images and the faster Image Flash 1.0 for video. It claims near-perfect scores on its own tests and 98.7% on the DF26 academic benchmark. Both are in its web app and API, and Image 1.0 is in its Chrome extension.

Pangram, a New York startup known for spotting AI-written text, has launched two models for detecting AI images and video, the company said on Wednesday. Pangram Image 1.0 checks still images, while the smaller, faster Pangram Image Flash 1.0 checks video frame by frame.

The image model can also draw a heatmap showing where AI content sits in a picture, such as a fake object added to a real photo. Pangram says it catches 99.2% of AI images and wrongly flags 0.27% of real ones.

In its own test, Pangram made AI copies of 100 real photos with 12 leading image generators, using prompts that closely matched each original. Image 1.0 scored 99.96% accuracy on that set. The test was designed by Pangram, not by outside reviewers.

For video, Pangram used DF26, an academic benchmark of 2,691 clips of people speaking to camera, published in September. Its authors found that both people and leading detectors did little better than chance at telling real clips from fakes.

Pangram says Flash 1.0 scores 98.7% on DF26, 29 points ahead of the next-best detector.

The new models also work on smaller images, down to 256 by 256 pixels from 512 by 512. Results below 512 pixels carry a lower-confidence warning. Pangram says it trains the models to see through compression, blur and noise, which platforms and people trying to fool detectors can add.

Image 1.0 now runs in Pangram’s Chrome extension, which flags AI content on social media, shopping and rental sites. Users can also upload videos in the web app, and the API now handles both images and video. Platforms are cracking down too: Snapchat has stopped recommending AI videos on Spotlight.

Pangram raised $9M in a round led by Menlo Ventures in July, when it first previewed image detection. Its text detector already runs on Substack.

AI detectors still make mistakes. In August, Pangram’s text tool flagged a Berkeley professor’s op-ed as partly AI-written, which she disputed.

Even Meta’s own image detector missed cropped fakes from its generator more than half the time in Reuters tests. On YouTube, a network that paid real actors to read AI scripts beat its detectors.

Democratic physicians, nurses, and scientists won dramatic victories across the U.S. on Tuesday, part of the sweeping blue wave that saw Democrats regain control of Congress in midterm elections that spotlighted  health care, public health, and trust in science.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Ally McCoist was critical of one Liverpool player for his unwitting role in Fenerbahce’s equaliser against the Reds in the first half of their Champions League game on Wednesday.

The away side took a well-deserved lead in the 25th minute when Trey Nyoni’s deflected shot sailed into the net, with the teenage midfielder being credited with his first official goal in senior football.

However, the hosts restored parity just four minutes later, and again it was with the aid of a deflection as Mason Greenwood’s free kick hit off Jeremy Jacquet and was diverted into the net, with Alisson Becker not disguising his rage at the defensive wall in front of him.

Speaking on co-commentary for TNT Sports (via BBC Sport), McCoist called out the French defender for turning his back on the ball as the set piece was struck.

The Scotsman said: “I think the goalkeeper will not be too happy with his wall. Alisson is livid. Jeremy Jacquet turns his back. You have to stand up and take it. Not good defending.”

The summer arrival from Rennes has been excellent in his time at Liverpool so far, rightly receiving plenty of praise for his performances and winning his first senior call-up for France, but he won’t be happy with his part in Greenwood’s rather fortuitous equaliser.

The normally teak-tough 21-year-old paid a heavy price for turning his back on the ball, even if nobody could legislate for the huge stroke of luck that Fenerbahce received.

He’s also met his physical match in Romelu Lukaku, with the Belgian striker standing up to Jacquet in a way that few (if any) Premier League forwards have managed since the start of this season.

If Liverpool were pegged back by a piece of brilliance which was near-impossible to defend, we’d be able to accept it, but the manner of Greenwood’s goal (and it had to be him, didn’t it?) will rankle as much with Andoni Iraola as it did with the visibly furious Alisson.

At the time of writing, hopefully the centre-back and his team can put that regrettable moment behind them and go on to take all three points home to Merseyside.

One Autism Mutation Collapses the Whole Gene Network

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A genetic investigation led by researchers at Boston Children’s Hospital and Harvard University resolves a longstanding puzzle in neurodevelopmental genomics: how hundreds of distinct genetic mutations can produce the shared biological features of autism. Published in Nature, the study reveals a phenomenon termed “transcriptomic convergence,” where mutating or downregulating a single autism risk gene triggers a coordinated downregulation across an entire network of related autism genes.

Furthermore, the team discovered that an X-linked transcriptional activator called ZFX, which escapes X-chromosome inactivation and is expressed at higher levels in females, acts as a master regulator of this convergent network—uncovering the molecular machinery underlying the female protective effect in autism.

Source: Boston Children’s Hospital / Nature

Over the past two decades, genomic sequencing projects have identified hundreds of rare genetic variants and de novo mutations associated with autism spectrum disorder. However, this vast genetic heterogeneity has posed a fundamental biological paradox: how can distinct disruptions scattered across hundreds of unrelated genes with vastly different molecular functions result in similar neurodevelopmental outcomes and shared behavioral traits?

Concurrently, clinicians and geneticists have grappled with the “female protective effect”, the consistent observation that autism is diagnosed four to five times more frequently in males than in females, and that females often require a substantially higher burden of deleterious genetic mutations before manifesting clinical symptoms.

Now, a study published in Nature by researchers at Boston Children’s Hospital, the Howard Hughes Medical Institute (HHMI), and Harvard Medical School delivers a unified explanation for both phenomena. The team demonstrated that autism genes do not operate in isolation but function within tightly interconnected transcriptional networks where single faults cascade, and identified an X-linked master activator that shields female neural cells from system collapse.

Led by senior author Christopher Walsh, MD, PhD, chief of the Division of Genetics and Genomics at Boston Children’s Hospital, alongside co-lead authors Rebecca Andersen, PhD, and Maya Talukdar, PhD, the researchers investigated human cellular models engineered to carry disruptions in individual, validated autism risk genes.

Rather than observing unique, disjointed transcriptional profiles for each genetic perturbation, the investigators observed a common signature across diverse variants:

“The point is, if you down-regulate one autism gene, you down-regulate a whole bunch of them,” said Dr. Andersen. “This ‘transcriptomic convergence,’ as we call it, gives us a way to understand how hundreds of different genes can cause similar phenotypes, a similar condition.”

This network fragility indicates that autism-related genes act in concert. Rather than requiring cumulative multi-gene hits, a single rare loss-of-function mutation can trigger a molecular domino effect that destabilizes an entire developmental program.

Having identified this interconnected network, the investigators sought to determine what molecular mechanisms might safeguard females from the cascade.

While human females carry two X chromosomes (XX) and males carry one (XY), cellular dosage compensation typically silences most genes on one female X chromosome through X-chromosome inactivation (XCI) to prevent toxic overexpression. However, roughly 15% of X-linked genes escape this silencing.

The team turned their attention to ZFX (zinc finger protein, X-linked), a known transcriptional activator residing on the X chromosome that reliably escapes inactivation. Because both copies remain functionally active, female cells express substantially higher baseline concentrations of the ZFX protein than male cells.

Molecular assays revealed that ZFX directly regulates and drives the expression of numerous genes embedded within the convergent autism network:

“The most important take-home messages are the idea of this transcriptomic convergence in autism and our identification of the ZFX gene as a potential driver of autism’s male bias,” said Dr. Walsh.

The identification of transcriptomic convergence and the ZFX regulatory axis fundamentally reframes the therapeutic horizon for neurodevelopmental conditions.

Historically, the presence of hundreds of unique autism-linked mutations suggested that drug developers would need to engineer hundreds of distinct gene-replacement or gene-editing therapies—a prohibitive challenge for clinical translation.

Because the network converges onto shared nodes, targeting master regulators like ZFX could offer a unified therapeutic strategy. Artificially elevating ZFX expression or activating its downstream targets in males could potentially confer the same neurodevelopmental resilience that females naturally possess. Furthermore, boosting ZFX could also benefit females carrying exceptionally high mutation loads.

As Dr. Walsh noted, developing interventions to amplify the expression of these convergent hubs could represent a therapeutic strategy where “a rising tide lifts all boats.”

What is transcriptomic convergence in autism?

Transcriptomic convergence is a biological phenomenon where disrupting or mutating a single autism-linked gene causes a widespread, coordinated downregulation across an entire network of other autism-associated genes. This explains how hundreds of different rare genetic mutations lead to similar neurobiological outcomes.

How does the ZFX gene protect females from autism?

ZFX is an X-linked transcriptional activator that escapes standard X-chromosome inactivation, meaning females express it at higher levels from both X chromosomes. Because ZFX directly drives the expression of many autism-associated genes, its higher concentration in females acts as a protective buffer, keeping the gene network stable even when a mutation is present.

What are the therapeutic implications of these findings?

Instead of creating individual gene therapies for hundreds of different autism mutations, researchers can focus on developing therapies that upregulate central hubs like ZFX. Boosting ZFX could potentially elevate the entire downstream network of autism genes, extending the natural female protective effect to males.

Funding: This work was supported by the Autism Speaks Postdoctoral Fellowship 13008 and NIGMS T32 GM007748 (R.E.A.); NIH T32 GM007753 and T32 GM144273 (M.T.); the Simons Foundation Autism Research Initiative, the Howard Hughes Medical Institute, Autism BrainNet (953759), the Yang Tan Collective at Harvard University, the Hock E. Tan and K. Lisa Yang Center for Autism Research at Harvard University. D.C.P. and C.A.W. are Investigators of the Howard Hughes Medical Institute.

Transcriptomic convergence and the female protective effect in autism

Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) is highly heritable, and mutations in hundreds of genes have been implicated as individually rare causes of ASD. Understanding how disruptions to these functionally diverse genes lead to the core features of ASD remains a major challenge.

Moreover, ASD is three- to four-fold more common in male than in female individuals, and female autistic people tend to carry more autosomal risk alleles for ASD compared with their male counterparts; however, the biological basis of this ‘female protective effect’ (FPE) has remained unknown.

Here we show that individual perturbations of 18 ASD genes in human neural progenitor cells converge on shared effects on gene expression, including widespread downregulation of other ASD genes. De novo reconstruction of a gene regulatory network enabled identification of central transcriptional regulators that drive this transcriptomic convergence, including prominent ASD gene CHD8 and candidates including REST.

Furthermore, X-linked transcription factor ZFX, which is expressed from both the active and the inactive X chromosomes in female individuals, emerged as a key activator of many ASD genes: we propose that the higher ZFX expression level observed in the female brain can buffer damaging mutations in diverse ASD genes, contributing to the female protective effect.

Together, these results show how key gene regulatory networks can become broadly and similarly dysregulated upon disruption of individual ASD genes and provide molecular insight into the female protective effect in ASD.

9 Easy (Enough) Ways to Toddler-Proof Your Christmas Tree

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

This one’s a no-brainer. Pack up your heirloom ornaments for the foreseeable future and invest in some chic shatterproof options. Think of it as a chance to temporarily lean into a fun trend, like a monochromatic vibe or a brightly colored artificial tree with fewer ornaments (make it extra fun by letting your kids pick the color—if they can agree, ha).

The good thing about toddlers is that they’re small, so simply placing a shorter tree up on a table is enough to keep those curious hands at bay. Make sure your platform is high enough that your tot can’t tug a branch and pull the whole tree down, and heavy enough that they can’t topple the table itself.

The other great thing about toddlers is that they’re easily duped, so if you wrap a few big boxes in festive paper and position them around the base of your tree, they look like the other presents that are hands-off until Christmas morning. Throw some heavy stuff in the boxes to keep them stable (anything you have lying around your house works). If you’re not as Pinterest-y as this sounds (same), throw a baby gate around the tree. (This one is actually not ugly!)

To keep your evergreen firmly planted on the ground, grab a few bricks (you can wrap them all pretty if you like) and place them on the legs or base of your tree to really hold it in place. This means if your toddler somehow makes a break for it, they won’t be able to tip it over.

Mentally place your ornaments in a pyramid of preciousness, with the most cherished at the top and the least delicate (those “charming” homemade or felted ones) at the bottom. This way, your tot’s curious little hands can only grab ornaments that won’t break or hurt them. You could also consider going all felt for a few years or only decorating the top of the tree.

Any toddler parent knows that when the house is too quiet, disaster looms, so tie some jingle bell ornaments to the lower branches to give you a heads-up if your kid’s made contact with the tree. Bonus: they look quite festive.

If the lower branches are the biggest liability, go ahead and chop them off.  Problem solved.

Check your older ornaments to see if any still hang with a sharp metal hook and update them with a loop of ribbon or ornament anchors.  That way, if your kiddo does get their hands on one of the shiny things hanging from the tree, you know they won’t accidentally poke themselves.

There are tons of tree options specifically made for toddlers if you’re OK giving up the greenery for a few years. Or rock two Christmas trees so your toddler has their own safe version to manhandle. Here are a few outside-the-box ideas:

If you ever went through a washi tape phase, create a cute and toddler-proof Christmas tree by taping the right shape to the wall (with no harm to your paint!). Kids can help decorate with ribbons of washi and round pieces of paper acting as ornaments. I know it’s not the same effect, but at least you’ll be able to rest easy.

If your house can somehow fit one more toy, add this Step 2 toddler Christmas tree to the mix. It’s the perfect size for your merry little munchkin; they can redecorate it as many times as they like, and there’s a fun train track going around the bottom so it’s a gift that keeps on giving.

If space is tight but you’ve got a free wall, this Project Montessori toddler-proof felt Christmas tree will keep them busy while you work on the main event. The felt cutout is about 3 feet tall, perfect for 3-year-olds who want to decorate their tree again and again and again and again—and with 26 Velcro ornaments, they’ll never run out of combinations.

Bringing a little more joy to parenthood.

United Airlines First Class Steak And Eggs: Great Steak, But Where Were The Eggs?

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

United Airlines served me excellent cubed steak for breakfast on a recent flight from Chicago to Houston. The problem with its steak and eggs dish? The missing eggs.

For this week’s Meal of the Week, I want to highlight a breakfast that I would happily order again, even though one part of it was very disappointing. On a recent flight from Chicago O’Hare (ORD) to Houston (IAH), I selected the steak and eggs, described on the menu as:

Steak and eggs with home fries and chimichurri sauce

The tray was nicely presented, with sliced steak topped with chimichurri, potatoes, and what I expected would be an egg soufflé.

The steak was excellent for airplane red meat. The chimichurri worked very well with the beef, and this was the highlight of the meal.

Unfortunately, instead of the egg soufflé I expected, I received something much closer to bread pudding…in fact, it was the same bread pudding I had out of Chicago a couple months ago. There may have been egg binding it together, but it had virtually no discernible egg texture or flavor. It was like eating bread.

You can see the bread cubes in the photo. This was not simply a soufflé that turned out a little firmer than expected. Bread dominated the portion that was supposed to provide the eggs in my steak and eggs.

That was disappointing, especially because the steak was so good and because I try to limit my carbs. I hope United can address this with its catering out of Chicago, as I’ve seen other reports of the same problem (this wasn’t a one-off). An actual egg component would make this a much more satisfying breakfast and match the menu…

The accompaniments were better. The chocolate croissant was served warm and was excellent. Between that and the bread pudding, I certainly had enough bread on the tray…

The fruit was ripe, and the Chobani vanilla Greek yogurt was decent. I would prefer a no-sugar-added Greek yogurt, but at least this one provided 10 grams of protein.

The coffee was strong, which was also appreciated. I also requested orange juice, though I must confess that was more to add color to the tray for the photo. I did not drink it.

I would order United’s steak and eggs again. The steak with chimichurri was excellent, and the warm chocolate croissant was great. But United needs to fix the egg issue out of ORD. There is a good breakfast here. It just needs the eggs its name promises.

My Meal of the Week feature examines an airline meal from my travels over the years. This may be a meal from earlier in the week or it may be a meal served over a decade ago.

Nintendo's main Switch cartridge supplier is leaving the ROM business

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

This is potentially terrible news for those who love physical media.

Nintendo's main Switch cartridges has plans to abandon the ROM portion of its business, according to reporting by the Taipei Times. The Taiwanese semiconductor and memory manufacturer Macronix is phasing out its ROM production, which was confirmed by CEO Miin Wu. This could spell bad news for gamers who love physical media.

Macronix just announced a record-high net profit of $337 million for Q3 2026, but Wu says many of its new customers have issued a "demand for NOR used in data centers." NOR is a type of flash memory.

1/4 Nintendo's main memory chip maker is quitting the Switch business Macronix, which makes the ROM chips in Switch 1 and Switch 2 game memory cards, is phasing out that business. There will be no new ROM products, and Q3 is the last quarter it reports ROM sales separately.

— David Gibson (@gibbogame) November 4, 2026

The company's ROM business has actually declined by 39 percent over the past year. This section of Macronix now accounts for just five percent of sales, down from 26 percent this time last year. In other words, the financials just aren't there.

There is a silver lining here. Macronix may be Nintendo's main supplier of ROM for its cartridges, but it's far from the only entity helping to create physical Switch cartridges. Also, this doesn't impact Switch 2 cartridges in quite the same way. That's because the Switch 2 carts use NAND flash memory, a market segment that Macronix isn't phasing out.

Sony PlayStation FY2025 Q4 Financial Results!

• PS5 units sold – 1.5M • Lifetime PS5 units – 93.7M • Full game software units – 74.6M • First party software units – 5.8M • Digital Download Ratio – 85% • Monthly Active Users – 125M pic.twitter.com/ARBwBehu6b

However, this doesn't look too good for the long-term sustainability of physical game cartridges and discs. Digital products already account for 61.5 percent of Nintendo's software sales. This is even starker when you look at the other console manufacturers. Digital downloads make up around 85 percent of software sales on the PS5, which could account for Sony's recent move to end physical media sales altogether.

Both of the above numbers pale in comparison to Microsoft and its Xbox division. A whopping 96 percent of software sales are digital on Xbox platforms. The company has still yet to announce a plan to sunset physical media.

Share of Adjustable Rate Mortgages Hits Highest Level Since September 2025

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

A house key sitting on mortgage application.To see more of my financial images click on the link below:

Mortgage rates ticked down a bit this past week but landing at 7.3%, it wasn’t enough to move the needle for buyers submitting loan applications for 30-year-fixed rate mortgages. Sitting almost a full percentage point lower, adjustable rate mortgages (ARMs) however continue to be attractive for buyers looking to start out with a lower monthly payment.

The latest Market Composite Index from the Mortgage Bankers Association (MBA)—its measure of mortgage loan activity volume and includes purchases and refinances—shows mortgage application activity decreasing 1.2% on a seasonally adjusted basis after last week’s 3.4% increase. On an unadjusted basis, the Index decreased 2% compared with the previous week.

This week’s decline follows weeks of fluctuations as the 30-year fixed mortgage rate still lingers around 7.29%, according to Freddie Mac and this week currently sits at 7.25%, according to Mortgage News Daily. Freddie Mac’s next rate report will be released Thursday.

“Mortgage rates drifted down to their lowest level since September 2026 last week in the wake of the Federal Reserve’s decision to hold on additional rate hikes at its October meeting,” said Mike Fratantoni, MBA’s SVP and chief economist. “While the 30-year rate declined to 7.3 percent, activity is still quite slow, with purchase application volume down for the week and refinance volume effectively flat at a very low level.”

MBA once again pointed to increased activity in the ARM share of total mortgage applications as a sign that buyers are ready to move on lower rates. According to the report, the average contract interest rate for 5/1 ARMs decreased to 6.29% from 6.41%, with points decreasing to 1.00 from 1.20 (including the origination fee) for 80 percent LTV loans. The adjustable-rate mortgage (ARM) share of activity increased to 11.4 percent of total applications, MBA reported.

“As we have highlighted in recent weeks, more borrowers are choosing ARMs in this environment to lower their initial payments, with the ARM share at its highest level since September 2025,” Fratantoni said.

An ARM loan is where the interest rate is fixed for an initial period, typically five years, and then changes annually for the life of the loan, based on market conditions.

MBA’s Refinance Index increased 0.1% from the previous week and was 60% lower than the same week one year ago. MBA’s seasonally adjusted Purchase Index–its weekly measurement of nationwide home loan applications based on a sample of about 75% of U.S. mortgage activity–decreased 2% from one week earlier. The unadjusted Purchase Index decreased 4% compared with the previous week and was 19% lower than the same week one year ago, according to the report.

The refinance share of mortgage activity saw an increase this week–to 39.8% from 38.9% the previous week, MBA reported.

A look at government-backed loans this week shows the FHA share of total applications decreased to 16.0% from 16.6% the week prior. The VA share of total applications increased to 11.2% from 11.0% the week prior, and the USDA share of total applications increased to 0.6% from 0.5% the week prior.
